```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes the statistical profile of text across a grid of 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\\times$ 4 generation depths (d0--d3), yielding 9 experimental configurations each spanning 4 depths.\nFor each cell we train a Random Forest (RF) classifier on the 19 statistical features defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method} and examine (i)~in-distribution depth classification accuracy, (ii)~which features carry depth signal, (iii)~how far into the recursion chain depth remains discriminable, and (iv)~how decoding strategy modulates the degradation trajectory.\n\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:indist}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports 4-class depth estimation accuracy under 5-fold cross-validation.\nAll 9 cells exceed chance (25\\%) by a wide margin, with accuracy ranging from 69.6\\% to 85.4\\%.\nBoth model family and decoding strategy influence accuracy, with model ordering GPT-2~XL $>$ OLMo $>$ Pythia holding across all strategies.\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trails by 6--9 percentage points, while temperature and nucleus sampling alternate for the lead depending on model family.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{In-distribution 4-class depth estimation accuracy (\\%) across 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies. RF classifier with 19 statistical features, 5-fold CV (std 0.2--0.9pp across all cells). Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Nucleus & Temp & Top-$k$ \\\\\n & $p{=}0.95$ & $\\tau{=}0.9$ & $k{=}50$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 83.3 & \\textbf{85.4} & 76.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBecause the human-written seed text (d0) is often trivially separable from any machine-generated depth, we also evaluate 3-class accuracy excluding d0.\nPerformance drops moderately---GPT-2~XL with temperature sampling still reaches 82.0\\%, and even the weakest setting (Pythia nucleus) retains 70.8\\%---confirming that the classifier captures inter-generation differences beyond the human--machine boundary.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nTo identify which statistical fingerprints carry depth signal, we train a dedicated binary RF per cell (d$_i$ vs.\\ rest) and extract Gini importances.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:atlas_heatmap} visualizes the top-7 feature importances across all 9 cells.\n\nTwo features dominate.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells, while surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal}) does so in 7 of 9.\nIn the two exceptions---GPT-2~XL with temperature and top-$k$ sampling---the 75th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p75\\_ppl}) replaces surprisal variance in the top 2, though surprisal variance remains among the top 4 features.\nTo quantify cross-model agreement, we compute the Spearman rank correlation of the full 19-feature importance vectors across all $\\binom{9}{2}=36$ cell pairs and obtain a mean $\\rho = 0.868$ (range: 0.619--0.954).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature importance heatmap across 9 cells (3 models $\\times$ 3 strategies). Top-7 features by mean Gini importance. \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} appears in the top 2 for all cells; \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} for 7 of 9.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThese results align with theoretical predictions: recursive finetuning erodes the tail of the token-probability distribution \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, and high-percentile perplexity directly measures tail behavior.\nThe universality of these markers across architectures and decoding strategies suggests they reflect a structural property of the recursive generation process rather than model-specific artifacts.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nAggregate accuracy masks the fact that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases.\nWe formalize this via the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD; defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}): $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the maximum depth $d^*$ such that the pairwise AUC between $d^*$ and $d^*{+}1$ exceeds a threshold $T$, using a dedicated binary RF per adjacent pair.\n\nWithin the 4-class atlas (d0--d3), 7 of 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=3$; the two exceptions are Pythia and OLMo with top-$k$ sampling ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=2$), consistent with top-$k$'s signal-attenuating effect noted in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:indist}.\n\nTo probe deeper, we extend the Pythia-1.4B nucleus pipeline to 6 depths (d0--d5) and train a 500-tree binary RF for each adjacent pair (we increase from 200 to 500 trees for binary classifiers to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair sample sizes).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} shows the pairwise AUC decay curve.\nThe d0\\,vs.\\,d1 boundary is near-perfect (AUC\\,=\\,0.998), reflecting the well-known human--machine gap.\nSubsequent boundaries degrade monotonically: d1\\,vs.\\,d2 achieves 0.925 [95\\% CI: 0.914,\\,0.935], d2\\,vs.\\,d3 reaches 0.823 [0.805,\\,0.840], and d3\\,vs.\\,d4 yields 0.784 [0.765,\\,0.802]---with the confidence interval lower bound still exceeding $T{=}0.75$.\nAt d4\\,vs.\\,d5, AUC drops to 0.683 [0.661,\\,0.705], falling below the threshold and fixing $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). Shaded bands: 95\\% bootstrap CI. Dashed line: $T{=}0.75$. $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$: depth pairs up to d3\\,vs.\\,d4 remain discriminable.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} shows the sensitivity of EDD to the choice of threshold.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all pairs through d4\\,vs.\\,d5 pass, giving $\\mathrm{EDD}=5$.\nAt $T{=}0.80$, only d1\\,vs.\\,d2 and d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pass, yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}=3$.\nThe d0\\,vs.\\,d1 pair passes at every threshold ($\\text{AUC}\\geq0.99$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{EDD sensitivity to threshold $T$ (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$). \\checkmark: pairwise AUC $\\geq T$; ---: below $T$. d0\\,vs.\\,d1 always passes (AUC$\\geq$0.99) and is omitted.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{cccccr}\n\\toprule\n$T$ & d1/d2 & d2/d3 & d3/d4 & d4/d5 & EDD \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.65 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & 5 \\\\\n0.70 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & 4 \\\\\n0.75 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & 4 \\\\\n0.80 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & --- & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity ablation.}\nDecision boundary dilution motivates EDD's binary-pair design: mean accuracy drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class), and the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 AUC falls from 0.804 to 0.689 between the 4-class and 6-class settings for Pythia nucleus---a 14\\% relative decline attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:trajectories}\n\nThe atlas reveals that decoding strategy does not merely scale the overall signal strength---it reshapes the degradation trajectory.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} plots normalized feature means across depths for three key features.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Normalized feature trajectories across generation depths for 3 models $\\times$ 2 strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$). All features decrease monotonically with depth, but inter-depth spacing is highly non-uniform. Top-$k$ results are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:topk_trajectories}.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-uniform inter-depth spacing.}\nFor Pythia nucleus, \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} drops from 483.7 (d0) to 61.4 (d1)---an 87\\% reduction---but only from 29.7 (d2) to 18.2 (d3), a 39\\% reduction.\nThis geometric compression of the feature space explains why adjacent-pair AUC decays monotonically (Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay}): each successive generation step yields diminishing statistical displacement.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced acceleration.}\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) applies uniform logit scaling rather than the hard truncation of nucleus or top-$k$, which accelerates convergence toward the low-entropy attractor described by \\citet{marchi2024_heat_death}.\nConcretely, feature values at depth $N$ under temperature sampling approximate those at depth $N{+}1$ under nucleus or top-$k$.\nThis shift yields higher in-distribution accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix}) because each depth occupies a more distinct region of feature space, but it also means the d1/d2 boundary under temperature is closer to the d2/d3 boundary under nucleus---a mismatch that creates reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\n\\paragraph{Three-tier strategy taxonomy.}\nThe trajectory data suggests a functional classification.\nNucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) serves as a stable baseline with moderate signal strength.\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) enhances the signal via accelerated convergence, yielding higher within-strategy accuracy but strategy-specific depth boundaries.\nTop-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) attenuates the signal---accuracy drops 6--9 points relative to nucleus---because hard truncation at a fixed vocabulary size preserves more of the original distribution's shape across depths, compressing inter-depth distances.\nNotably, top-$k$ preserves the \\emph{direction} of all feature trends despite the magnitude reduction, which has implications for ordinal classifiers that rely on monotonicity (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).",
```

```json
{

    "oldString": "ranking in the top two features by Gini importance in 9/9 and 7/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.",
    "newString": "ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 7/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical---it is already occurring in the wild---yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all generation depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking schemes \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} require proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively to existing corpora.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} characterizes how recursive training degrades model quality but assumes the generation depth is known a priori---the very quantity we seek to estimate.\n\\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} show that filtering synthetic data from training corpora can prevent collapse, yet their approach relies on binary detection and does not distinguish among recursive depths.\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates the extent of single-pass editing applied to a text but does not address multi-step recursive generation.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nOur key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints exhibit universal stability across model families and decoding strategies.\nSpecifically, perplexity tail statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by Gini importance in 9/9 and 7/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.\n\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.\nGiven a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).\nTo systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, distinguishing it from binary detection and single-pass editing estimation. We introduce the EDD metric, which summarizes the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain separable above a given AUC threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} We construct a systematic $3 \\times 3$ map across model families and decoding strategies, revealing that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells (100\\%) and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9 (78\\%), with a mean Spearman rank correlation of $\\rho{=}0.868$ across importance vectors. The extended Pythia nucleus pipeline (d0--d5) yields $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$, establishing that depth discrimination persists through four recursive steps before falling below threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization analysis.} Cross-model leave-one-out transfer achieves 66.4\\% mean accuracy and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\% (chance = 25\\%), demonstrating partial universality of depth fingerprints. We identify a temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion (GPT-2~XL: 0.13, OLMo: 0.25) caused by accelerated convergence. Z-score normalization recovers logistic regression performance for non-temperature holdouts (d1v2 AUC 0.61--0.89) but fails to resolve the temperature-specific inversion, revealing a two-layer covariate shift structure.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning as negative result.} Deep Contrastive Ordinal Learning (DCOL) with RoBERTa-large, tested across 6 configurations, achieves at best 64.5\\% versus 78.2\\% for feature-based classifiers on the same data, with 5 of 6 runs failing due to embedding collapse, OOM, or NaN losses. This suggests that explicit statistical features capture depth-relevant information that end-to-end models struggle to extract from raw text.\n\\end{itemize}",
```

```json
    "oldString": "\\caption{Feature importance heatmap across 9 cells (3 models $\\times$ 3 strategies). Top-7 features by mean Gini importance. \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} appears in the top 2 for all cells; \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} for 7 of 9.}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{The Depth Degradation Atlas}\n\\label{sec:atlas}\n\nWe systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes the statistical profile of text across a grid of 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies $\\times$ 4 generation depths (d0--d3), yielding 9 experimental configurations each spanning 4 depths.\nFor each cell we train a Random Forest (RF) classifier on the 19 statistical features defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method} and examine (i)~in-distribution depth classification accuracy, (ii)~which features carry depth signal, (iii)~how far into the recursion chain depth remains discriminable, and (iv)~how decoding strategy modulates the degradation trajectory.\n\n\\subsection{In-Distribution Performance}\n\\label{sec:atlas:indist}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_matrix} reports 4-class depth estimation accuracy under 5-fold cross-validation.\nAll 9 cells exceed chance (25\\%) by a wide margin, with accuracy ranging from 69.6\\% to 85.4\\%.\nBoth model family and decoding strategy influence accuracy, with model ordering GPT-2~XL $>$ OLMo $>$ Pythia holding across all strategies.\nTop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) trails by 6--9 percentage points, while temperature and nucleus sampling alternate for the lead depending on model family.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{In-distribution 4-class depth estimation accuracy (\\%) across 3 model families $\\times$ 3 decoding strategies. RF classifier with 19 statistical features, 5-fold CV (std 0.2--0.9pp across all cells). Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:main_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nModel & Nucleus & Temp & Top-$k$ \\\\\n & $p{=}0.95$ & $\\tau{=}0.9$ & $k{=}50$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-1.4B & 78.2 & 76.3 & 69.6 \\\\\nOLMo-1B & 78.8 & 82.0 & 74.2 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 83.3 & \\textbf{85.4} & 76.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBecause the human-written seed text (d0) is often trivially separable from any machine-generated depth, we also evaluate 3-class accuracy excluding d0.\nPerformance drops moderately---GPT-2~XL with temperature sampling still reaches 82.0\\%, and even the weakest setting (Pythia nucleus) retains 70.8\\%---confirming that the classifier captures inter-generation differences beyond the human--machine boundary.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Universality}\n\\label{sec:atlas:features}\n\nTo identify which statistical fingerprints carry depth signal, we train a dedicated binary RF per cell (d$_i$ vs.\\ rest) and extract feature importances (Gini impurity; permutation importance yields consistent rankings, Appendix~\\ref{app:permutation}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:atlas_heatmap} visualizes the top-7 feature importances across all 9 cells.\n\nTwo features dominate.\nThe 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells, while surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal}) does so in 7 of 9.\nIn the two exceptions---GPT-2~XL with temperature and top-$k$ sampling---the 75th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p75\\_ppl}) replaces surprisal variance in the top 2, though surprisal variance remains among the top 4 features.\nTo quantify cross-model agreement, we compute the Spearman rank correlation of the full 19-feature importance vectors across all $\\binom{9}{2}=36$ cell pairs and obtain a mean $\\rho = 0.868$ (range: 0.619--0.954).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_atlas_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Feature importance heatmap across 9 cells (3 models $\\times$ 3 strategies). Top-7 features by mean Gini importance. \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} appears in the top 2 for all cells; \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} for 7 of 9.}\n\\label{fig:atlas_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThese results align with theoretical predictions: recursive finetuning erodes the tail of the token-probability distribution \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}, and high-percentile perplexity directly measures tail behavior.\nThe universality of these markers across architectures and decoding strategies suggests they reflect a structural property of the recursive generation process rather than model-specific artifacts.\n\n\\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}\n\\label{sec:atlas:edd}\n\nAggregate accuracy masks the fact that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases.\nWe formalize this via the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD; defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}): $\\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the maximum depth $d^*$ such that the pairwise AUC between $d^*$ and $d^*{+}1$ exceeds a threshold $T$, using a dedicated binary RF per adjacent pair.\n\nWithin the 4-class atlas (d0--d3), 7 of 9 cells achieve $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=3$; the two exceptions are Pythia and OLMo with top-$k$ sampling ($\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=2$), consistent with top-$k$'s signal-attenuating effect noted in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas:indist}.\n\nTo probe deeper, we extend the Pythia-1.4B nucleus pipeline to 6 depths (d0--d5) and train a 500-tree binary RF for each adjacent pair (we increase from 200 to 500 trees for binary classifiers to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair sample sizes).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay} shows the pairwise AUC decay curve.\nThe d0\\,vs.\\,d1 boundary is near-perfect (AUC\\,=\\,0.998), reflecting the well-known human--machine gap.\nSubsequent boundaries degrade monotonically: d1\\,vs.\\,d2 achieves 0.925 [95\\% CI: 0.914,\\,0.935], d2\\,vs.\\,d3 reaches 0.823 [0.805,\\,0.840], and d3\\,vs.\\,d4 yields 0.784 [0.765,\\,0.802]---with the confidence interval lower bound still exceeding $T{=}0.75$.\nAt d4\\,vs.\\,d5, AUC drops to 0.683 [0.661,\\,0.705], falling below the threshold and fixing $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_auc_decay.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). Shaded bands: 95\\% bootstrap CI. Dashed line: $T{=}0.75$. $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$: depth pairs up to d3\\,vs.\\,d4 remain discriminable.}\n\\label{fig:auc_decay}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} shows the sensitivity of EDD to the choice of threshold.\nAt $T{=}0.65$, all pairs through d4\\,vs.\\,d5 pass, giving $\\mathrm{EDD}=5$.\nAt $T{=}0.80$, only d1\\,vs.\\,d2 and d2\\,vs.\\,d3 pass, yielding $\\mathrm{EDD}=3$.\nThe d0\\,vs.\\,d1 pair passes at every threshold ($\\text{AUC}\\geq0.99$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{EDD sensitivity to threshold $T$ (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$). \\checkmark: pairwise AUC $\\geq T$; ---: below $T$. d0\\,vs.\\,d1 always passes (AUC$\\geq$0.99) and is omitted.}\n\\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{cccccr}\n\\toprule\n$T$ & d1/d2 & d2/d3 & d3/d4 & d4/d5 & EDD \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.65 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & 5 \\\\\n0.70 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & 4 \\\\\n0.75 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & 4 \\\\\n0.80 & \\checkmark & \\checkmark & --- & --- & 3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Granularity ablation.}\nDecision boundary dilution motivates EDD's binary-pair design: mean accuracy drops from 99.5\\% (binary) to 78.3\\% (4-class) to 62.9\\% (6-class), and the d2\\,vs.\\,d3 AUC falls from 0.804 to 0.689 between the 4-class and 6-class settings for Pythia nucleus---a 14\\% relative decline attributable to boundary dilution, not signal loss.\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Trajectories}\n\\label{sec:atlas:trajectories}\n\nThe atlas reveals that decoding strategy does not merely scale the overall signal strength---it reshapes the degradation trajectory.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:feature_trajectory} plots normalized feature means across depths for three key features.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_feature_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Normalized feature trajectories across generation depths for 3 models $\\times$ 2 strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$). All features decrease monotonically with depth, but inter-depth spacing is highly non-uniform. Top-$k$ results are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:topk_trajectories}.}\n\\label{fig:feature_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-uniform inter-depth spacing.}\nFor Pythia nucleus, \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} drops from 483.7 (d0) to 61.4 (d1)---an 87\\% reduction---but only from 29.7 (d2) to 18.2 (d3), a 39\\% reduction.\nThis geometric compression of the feature space explains why adjacent-pair AUC decays monotonically (Figure~\\ref{fig:auc_decay}): each successive generation step yields diminishing statistical displacement.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced acceleration.}\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) applies uniform logit scaling rather than the hard truncation of nucleus or top-$k$, which accelerates convergence toward the low-entropy attractor described by \\citet{marchi2024_heat_death}.\nConcretely, feature values at depth $N$ under temperature sampling approximate those at depth $N{+}1$ under nucleus or top-$k$.\nThis shift yields higher in-distribution accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:main_matrix}) because each depth occupies a more distinct region of feature space, but it also means the d1/d2 boundary under temperature is closer to the d2/d3 boundary under nucleus---a mismatch that creates reversal risk in cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\n\n\\paragraph{Three-tier strategy taxonomy.}\nThe trajectory data suggests a functional classification.\nNucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$) serves as a stable baseline with moderate signal strength.\nTemperature sampling ($\\tau{=}0.9$) enhances the signal via accelerated convergence, yielding higher within-strategy accuracy but strategy-specific depth boundaries.\nTop-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) attenuates the signal---accuracy drops 6--9 points relative to nucleus---because hard truncation at a fixed vocabulary size preserves more of the original distribution's shape across depths, compressing inter-depth distances.\nNotably, top-$k$ preserves the \\emph{direction} of all feature trends despite the magnitude reduction, which has implications for ordinal classifiers that rely on monotonicity (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).",
```

```json
    "oldString": "    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning as negative result.} Deep Contrastive Ordinal Learning (DCOL) with RoBERTa-large, tested across 6 configurations, achieves at best 64.5\\% versus 78.2\\% for feature-based classifiers on the same data, with 5 of 6 runs failing due to embedding collapse, OOM, or NaN losses. This suggests that explicit statistical features capture depth-relevant information that end-to-end models struggle to extract from raw text.",
    "newString": "    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning as negative result.} Both DeBERTa-v3-base (58.3\\%) and RoBERTa-base (59.5\\%) achieve ${\\sim}$59\\% via end-to-end finetuning---19pp below feature-based classifiers (78.2\\%)---with the architecture-invariant gap ruling out model selection bias. Probing reveals a significant negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.48$) between transformer attention and RF feature importance, confirming complementary representations.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nThe text on which large language models are trained increasingly contains their own outputs.\nAs synthetic content proliferates across the web, a recursive loop emerges: models trained on web corpora ingest text generated by earlier models, and their outputs feed into the next generation's training data \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,alemohammad2024_mad}.\nThis model$\\to$data$\\to$model cycle is no longer hypothetical---it is already occurring in the wild---yet we lack the ability to determine how many recursive iterations a given text has undergone, a distinction that matters because first-generation synthetic text may be benign or even beneficial for training, while deeply recursive text drives the distributional collapse that degrades model quality \\cite{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails}.\n\nExisting tools address adjacent but distinct problems.\nBinary detectors such as Binoculars \\cite{binoculars2024}, Fast-DetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt2024}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026} classify text as human-written or machine-generated, collapsing all generation depths $d \\geq 1$ into a single category.\nWatermarking schemes \\cite{kirchenbauer2023_watermark} require proactive embedding and cannot be applied retroactively to existing corpora.\nThe model collapse literature \\cite{shumailov2024_nature,dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} characterizes how recursive training degrades model quality but assumes the generation depth is known a priori---the very quantity we seek to estimate.\n\\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} show that filtering synthetic data from training corpora can prevent collapse, yet their approach relies on binary detection and does not distinguish among recursive depths.\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates the extent of single-pass editing applied to a text but does not address multi-step recursive generation.\nNo existing method estimates the ordinal recursive depth of a text passage from its content alone.\n\nOur key insight is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints in text, and these fingerprints exhibit universal stability across model families and decoding strategies.\nSpecifically, perplexity tail statistics---the 90th-percentile perplexity (\\textsc{p90\\_ppl}) and surprisal variance (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal})---emerge as dominant depth indicators, ranking in the top two features by importance in 9/9 and 7/9 model--strategy configurations, respectively.\n\nWe formalize recursive generation depth estimation as an ordinal classification task and introduce a lightweight pipeline to solve it.\nGiven a text passage, we compute a 19-dimensional feature vector from reference-model perplexity statistics---capturing distributional location, spread, tail behavior, and lexical diversity---and classify it with a Random Forest into one of $K{+}1$ depth classes ($d{=}0$ for human text through $d{=}K$ for $K$-th generation text).\nTo systematically characterize how recursive generation reshapes these features, we construct the \\emph{Depth Degradation Atlas}: a $3 \\times 3$ grid of model families (Pythia-1.4B, OLMo-1B, GPT-2~XL) and decoding strategies (nucleus $p{=}0.95$, temperature $\\tau{=}0.9$, top-$k$ $k{=}50$), each spanning 4 generation depths, totaling 180K samples.\nThe atlas reveals strategy-dependent degradation trajectories---temperature sampling accelerates convergence toward a low-entropy attractor, while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---and we introduce the \\emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric to quantify how far into the recursion chain adjacent depths remain distinguishable.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formalization.} We define recursive generation depth estimation as ordinal classification, distinguishing it from binary detection and single-pass editing estimation. We introduce the EDD metric, which summarizes the maximum depth at which adjacent generations remain separable above a given AUC threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Depth Degradation Atlas.} We construct a systematic $3 \\times 3$ map across model families and decoding strategies, revealing that \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} ranks in the top 2 in all 9 cells (100\\%) and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} in 7/9 (78\\%), with a mean Spearman rank correlation of $\\rho{=}0.868$ across importance vectors. The extended Pythia nucleus pipeline (d0--d5) yields $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$, establishing that depth discrimination persists through four recursive steps before falling below threshold.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Generalization analysis.} Cross-model leave-one-out transfer achieves 66.4\\% mean accuracy and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\% (chance = 25\\%), demonstrating partial universality of depth fingerprints. We identify a temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion (GPT-2~XL: 0.13, OLMo: 0.25) caused by accelerated convergence. Z-score normalization recovers logistic regression performance for non-temperature holdouts (d1v2 AUC 0.61--0.89) but fails to resolve the temperature-specific inversion, revealing a two-layer covariate shift structure.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{End-to-end learning as negative result.} Deep Contrastive Ordinal Learning (DCOL) with RoBERTa-large, tested across 6 configurations, achieves at best 64.5\\% versus 78.2\\% for feature-based classifiers on the same data, with 5 of 6 runs failing due to embedding collapse, OOM, or NaN losses. This suggests that explicit statistical features capture depth-relevant information that end-to-end models struggle to extract from raw text.\n\\end{itemize}",
```

```json
    "newString": "end-to-end finetuning's failure (${\\sim}$59\\% for both DeBERTa and RoBERTa vs.\\ feature-based 78.2\\%) underscore both the problem's difficulty and the effectiveness of explicit statistical features.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced recursive generation depth estimation and demonstrated that 19 lightweight statistical features suffice for 70--85\\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\\%).\nThe Depth Degradation Atlas reveals universal depth markers (p90 perplexity, surprisal variance) alongside strategy-dependent trajectories, with $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$.\nCross-model transfer reaches 66.4\\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\\%; the temperature-induced d1/d2 inversion and DCOL's failure (best 64.5\\% vs.\\ feature-based 78.2\\%) underscore both the problem's difficulty and the effectiveness of explicit statistical features.\nFuture work should address wild-data transfer, larger model scales, strategy-conditioned classifiers, and empirical validation of depth-graduated data curation.\nCode, data, and trained classifiers will be released upon acceptance.\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll experiments use Wikipedia seed text, LoRA finetuning, and 1--1.5B parameter models; transfer to mixed-domain text, instruction-tuned models, and larger scales (7B+) remains open.\nFeatures are computed relative to GPT-2 base (124M), introducing partial overlap when GPT-2~XL is held out, though GPT-2~XL holdout accuracy (67.6\\%) does not exceed the unrelated OLMo (69.2\\%).\nDetection features degrade under domain shift \\cite{pudasaini2026_detection_fails}; our depth features are likely susceptible.\nAdditionally, \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} find that early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality, potentially violating the monotonicity assumption, and \\citet{falahati2026_curated} show that curated synthetic data need not trigger collapse, attenuating the fingerprints we exploit.",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KhUpzGf5F7sUv8rHHQj8Wk`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	As large language models increasingly train on web text containing their own outputs, a recursive model$\to$data$\to$model loop arises---yet no method estimates how many such iterations a text has undergone.
3	We formalize \emph{recursive generation depth estimation} as ordinal classification and construct a benchmark of 180K passages spanning 3 model families, 3 decoding strategies, and 4 generation depths.
4	From each passage we extract 19 statistical features---perplexity percentiles, surprisal moments, and lexical diversity measures---computed against a fixed reference model (GPT-2 base, 124M).
5	A Random Forest classifier achieves 70--85\% four-class accuracy (chance = 25\%; a zero-shot LLM baseline reaches only 40\%), with 90th-percentile perplexity and surprisal variance emerging as universal depth markers (top-2 importance in 9/9 and 7/9 model--strategy cells, respectively; mean Spearman $\rho{=}0.868$).
6	Our \emph{Depth Degradation Atlas} reveals that decoding strategy reshapes degradation trajectories rather than merely attenuating them, and we define the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD) metric, finding $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}{=}4$ for extended recursion chains.
7	Cross-model transfer reaches 66.4\% and cross-strategy transfer 59.1\%, though temperature sampling induces a d1/d2 AUC inversion that z-score normalization cannot resolve.
8	\end{abstract}
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 2171 · 2026-05-15T13:32:16.963Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KAg3o3zRnDSyoXd3KVRh2T`</summary>

```text
111	\subsection{End-to-End Learning}
112	\label{sec:gen:dcol}
113	
114	We test whether end-to-end finetuning can bypass feature engineering.
115	We train DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base with standard hyperparameters (lr$\,{=}\,2{\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, batch 16, max 512 tokens, 5-fold CV) on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.
116	Both achieve ${\sim}$59\% accuracy (DeBERTa 58.3$\pm$4.7\%, RoBERTa 59.5$\pm$3.2\%), substantially below feature-based classifiers (78.2\%, gap ${\approx}$19pp).
117	Both transformers easily distinguish human from machine text (d0 F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42), precisely where statistical features like \textsc{p90\_ppl} and \textsc{var\_surprisal} excel.
118	The architecture-invariant gap (1.2pp between backbones) rules out model selection bias and confirms that hand-crafted statistical features capture depth-discriminative signals that end-to-end finetuning cannot recover from raw text alone.
119	
120	We additionally tested Deep Contrastive Ordinal Learning (DCOL) with a RoBERTa-large encoder; across six configurations, five suffered training instabilities (embedding collapse, OOM, gradient explosion) and the best run reached only 64.5\% with severe overfitting.
121	While ordinal contrastive methods such as CLOC \cite{cloc2025} show promise for continuous ordinal targets, the discrete and compressing nature of recursive depth resists end-to-end extraction.
122	
123	\paragraph{Feature overlap analysis.}
124	Probing analysis reveals a significant negative correlation between DeBERTa attention-based feature importance and RF permutation importance (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$), with zero overlap in top-5 features (Jaccard$\,{=}\,0.0$).
125	DeBERTa attends to surface-level lexical patterns (type--token ratio, repeated bigrams, bigram entropy), while RF relies on perplexity distribution statistics (\textsc{var\_surprisal}, \textsc{p90\_ppl}, skewness).
126	This complementarity confirms that transformer representations are biased toward lexical and repetition patterns, missing the high-order perplexity tail statistics that carry depth signal.
```

</details>

```json
    "oldString": "We test whether end-to-end finetuning can bypass feature engineering.\nWe train DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base with standard hyperparameters (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, batch 16, max 512 tokens, 5-fold CV) on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth achieve ${\\sim}$59\\% accuracy (DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%, RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%), substantially below feature-based classifiers (78.2\\%, gap ${\\approx}$19pp).\nBoth transformers easily distinguish human from machine text (d0 F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42), precisely where statistical features like \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} excel.\nThe architecture-invariant gap (1.2pp between backbones) rules out model selection bias and confirms that hand-crafted statistical features capture depth-discriminative signals that end-to-end finetuning cannot recover from raw text alone.\n\nWe additionally tested Deep Contrastive Ordinal Learning (DCOL) with a RoBERTa-large encoder; across six configurations, five suffered training instabilities (embedding collapse, OOM, gradient explosion) and the best run reached only 64.5\\% with severe overfitting.\nWhile ordinal contrastive methods such as CLOC \\cite{cloc2025} show promise for continuous ordinal targets, the discrete and compressing nature of recursive depth resists end-to-end extraction.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature overlap analysis.}\nProbing analysis reveals a significant negative correlation between DeBERTa attention-based feature importance and RF permutation importance (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$), with zero overlap in top-5 features (Jaccard$\\,{=}\\,0.0$).\nDeBERTa attends to surface-level lexical patterns (type--token ratio, repeated bigrams, bigram entropy), while RF relies on perplexity distribution statistics (\\textsc{var\\_surprisal}, \\textsc{p90\\_ppl}, skewness).\nThis complementarity confirms that transformer representations are biased toward lexical and repetition patterns, missing the high-order perplexity tail statistics that carry depth signal.",
    "newString": "We train DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nBoth achieve ${\\sim}$59\\% (DeBERTa 58.3$\\pm$4.7\\%, RoBERTa 59.5$\\pm$3.2\\%), 19pp below feature-based classifiers (78.2\\%).\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42), precisely where \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} excel.\nThe 1.2pp inter-backbone gap rules out model selection bias.\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} with RoBERTa-large fared worse (best 64.5\\%, 5/6 runs unstable).\nProbing reveals a negative correlation between DeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance ($\\rho{=}{-}0.48$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard$\\,{=}\\,0$): transformers attend to lexical patterns (TTR, repeated bigrams) while RF relies on perplexity tail statistics, confirming complementary---not redundant---representations.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nThe atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) established in-distribution accuracy of 69.6--85.4\\%.\nWe now test whether these results reflect genuine statistical regularities or artifacts of specific training conditions, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learned representations (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:dcol}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers (tree ensemble, ordinal regression, diversity-based) achieve identical performance on the same features confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 statistical features, not the classifier architecture.\nA zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) achieves 40.0\\%---above chance but 38 points below feature-based methods.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows that d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; in-distribution errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary, while cross-model transfer spreads confusion more broadly across d1--d3.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nTo test whether depth fingerprints generalize across architectures, we perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two model families (nucleus sampling only) and test on the held-out third.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reports results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance).\nThe per-class breakdown reveals that d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates headline accuracy; d2 recall drops as low as 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\nPythia is the hardest holdout (62.4\\%) and OLMo the easiest (69.2\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe next train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports per-model results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576).\nThis follows from the trajectory analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}: temperature sampling shifts feature values at depth $d$ toward depth $d{+}1$ under nucleus/top-$k$.\n\n\\paragraph{Z-score normalization does not resolve the inversion.}\nA natural hypothesis is that per-feature z-score normalization might correct this covariate shift \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting}.\nFor RF, z-normalization leaves accuracy essentially unchanged (59.1\\% $\\to$ 59.2\\%) and does not improve d1v2 AUC ($\\Delta < 0.02$), as expected since tree splits depend on feature rank order, not scale.\nZ-score standardization substantially improves logistic regression cross-strategy transfer for nucleus and top-$k$ holdouts (d1v2 AUC 0.61--0.89), but fails to resolve the temperature-induced inversion (d1v2 AUC remains near or below chance: Pythia 0.52, GPT-2~XL 0.24, OLMo 0.19).\nThis two-layer structure---scale shift addressable by normalization, plus a deeper geometric shift specific to temperature sampling---suggests that temperature decoding induces qualitatively different feature distributions that cannot be reconciled by linear rescaling.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:dcol}\n\nWe test whether end-to-end finetuning can bypass feature engineering.\nWe train DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base with standard hyperparameters (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, batch 16, max 512 tokens, 5-fold CV) on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus easily distinguish human from machine text (d0 F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42), precisely where statistical features like \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} excel.\nThe architecture-invariant gap (1.2pp between backbones) rules out model selection bias and confirms that hand-crafted statistical features capture ```text
```
```text
4	\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}
5	\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursively training language models on their own outputs erodes distributional tails, eventually collapsing the output distribution to a narrow mode.
6	\citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this phenomenon through scaling laws that distinguish tail \emph{narrowing} (reduced variance) from tail \emph{cutting} (lost support), showing both accelerate with recursion depth; \citet{dohmatob2024_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and \citet{dohmatob2024_demystified} analyze the regression case.
7	The MAD framework \cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends this analysis to self-consuming generative models and quantifies the precision--recall tradeoff as synthetic data accumulates across generations.
8	\citet{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate} show that retaining real data alongside synthetic data across generations mitigates collapse, while \citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} demonstrate that filtering synthetic samples via binary detection can preserve model quality.
9	Recent work further refines the theoretical picture: \citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} identify non-monotonic dynamics where early recursion can temporarily \emph{improve} model quality before eventual degradation; \citet{marchi2024_heat_death} characterize convergence to a fixed-point ``heat death'' distribution; and \citet{wang2026_contamination} derive contamination guarantees bounding how much synthetic data a corpus can absorb.
10	Additional lines of work address curated synthetic pipelines \cite{falahati2026_curated}, knowledge collapse in factual recall \cite{knowledge_collapse2025}, spectral signatures of model-generated text \cite{sigma2026_spectral}, and the ``synthetic eggs'' problem in self-improving loops \cite{synthetic_eggs2026}.
11	These works characterize degradation patterns but assume the generation depth is known a priori; our work addresses the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.
```

```json
  "old_string": "\\citet{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate} show that retaining real data alongside synthetic data across generations mitigates collapse, while \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} demonstrate that filtering synthetic samples via binary detection can preserve model quality.\nRecent work further refines the theoretical picture: \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} identify non-monotonic dynamics where early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality before eventual degradation; \\citet{marchi2024_heat_death} characterize convergence to a fixed-point ``heat death'' distribution; and \\citet{wang2026_contamination} derive contamination guarantees bounding how much synthetic data a corpus can absorb.\nAdditional lines of work address curated synthetic pipelines \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, knowledge collapse in factual recall \\cite{knowledge_collapse2025}, spectral signatures of model-generated text \\cite{sigma2026_spectral}, and the ``synthetic eggs'' problem in self-improving loops \\cite{synthetic_eggs2026}.",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Model Collapse Theory.}\n\\citet{shumailov2024_nature} demonstrate that recursively training language models on their own outputs erodes distributional tails, eventually collapsing the output distribution to a narrow mode.\n\\citet{dohmatob2024_tale_of_tails} formalize this phenomenon through scaling laws that distinguish tail \\emph{narrowing} (reduced variance) from tail \\emph{cutting} (lost support), showing both accelerate with recursion depth; \\citet{dohmatob2024_strong_collapse} establish strong collapse guarantees, and \\citet{dohmatob2024_demystified} analyze the regression case.\nThe MAD framework \\cite{alemohammad2024_mad} extends this analysis to self-consuming generative models and quantifies the precision--recall tradeoff as synthetic data accumulates across generations.\n\\citet{gerstgrasser2024_accumulate} show that retaining real data alongside synthetic data across generations mitigates collapse, while \\citet{drayson2025_detection_prevents_collapse} demonstrate that filtering synthetic samples via binary detection can preserve model quality.\nRecent work further refines the theoretical picture: \\citet{bakshi2026_collapse_improvement} identify non-monotonic dynamics where early recursion can temporarily \\emph{improve} model quality before eventual degradation; \\citet{marchi2024_heat_death} characterize convergence to a fixed-point ``heat death'' distribution; and \\citet{wang2026_contamination} derive contamination guarantees bounding how much synthetic data a corpus can absorb.\nAdditional lines of work address curated synthetic pipelines \\cite{falahati2026_curated}, knowledge collapse in factual recall \\cite{knowledge_collapse2025}, spectral signatures of model-generated text \\cite{sigma2026_spectral}, and the ``synthetic eggs'' problem in self-improving loops \\cite{synthetic_eggs2026}.\nThese works characterize degradation patterns but assume the generation depth is known a priori; our work addresses the inverse problem of estimating depth from text alone.\n\n\\paragraph{AI Text Detection.}\nDetectGPT \\cite{mitchell2023_detectgpt} introduced probability curvature---the tendency of model-generated text to occupy local maxima of the log-probability surface---as a zero-shot detection signal.\n\\citet{fast_detectgpt2024} improve computational efficiency by replacing random perturbations with conditional probability curvature from a surrogate model.\nBinoculars \\cite{binoculars2024} exploits the perplexity ratio between two related models, achieving strong detection without training data.\nDivEye \\cite{diveye2026} leverages surprisal diversity patterns, while GLTR \\cite{gehrmann2019_gltr} provides statistical visualization of per-token likelihood ranks for human inspection.\n\\citet{sadasivan2023_paraphrase} expose fundamental limits: paraphrasing attacks reduce detection accuracy to near chance, and \\citet{pudasaini2026_detection_fails} show detection degrades under distribution shift between training and deployment domains.\n\\citet{pu2026_generator_barrier} formalize a generator barrier bounding detection performance when the generator is unknown.\nAll these methods perform binary classification (human vs.\\ machine), discarding the ordinal depth information that distinguishes first-generation from deeply recursive text.\nOur approach outputs ordinal depth labels, complementing rather than replacing binary detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Adjacent Tasks.}\nEditLens \\cite{editlens2026} estimates the \\emph{extent} of editing applied to a passage---a continuous measure of how much a text has been modified in a single pass---but does not model recursive generation chains.\n\\citet{uchendu2020_attribution} formulate authorship attribution as multi-source classification, identifying \\emph{which} model generated a text rather than \\emph{how many} recursive steps it has undergone.\nLOKI \\cite{loki2025} provides a comprehensive benchmark spanning multiple synthetic data modalities but evaluates binary detection, not depth estimation.\n\\citet{mohamed2025_broken_telephone} study iterative generation as a ``broken telephone'' effect, quantifying information distortion across rounds; while their work measures forward degradation fidelity, our task performs the inverse: classifying generation depth from text alone, without access to the generation chain.\n\n\\paragraph{Decoding Strategy Effects.}\nThe choice of decoding strategy shapes the statistical properties of generated text in ways that interact with recursive depth signals.\n\\citet{holtzman2020_nucleus} show that unrestricted sampling produces degenerate repetitions and propose nucleus sampling to maintain output diversity.\n\\citet{finlayson2024_closing} demonstrate that decoding parameters affect downstream evaluation metrics in non-trivial ways.\n\\citet{garces_arias2025_decoding_decoded} find that the effect of decoding strategies on text quality is model-dependent, complicating universal quality claims.\n\\citet{wiher2022_decoding} provide a systematic comparison of decoding methods on surface-level text properties, and Mirostat \\cite{basu2021_mirostat} introduces perplexity-controlled decoding that actively regulates output entropy.\nOur Depth Degradation Atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) reveals that these strategies induce qualitatively different degradation trajectories under recursive generation---temperature sampling accelerates convergence to a low-entropy attractor while top-$k$ attenuates the depth signal---effects that go beyond single-pass text quality to shape the discriminability of recursive depth itself.",
```

```text
56	\subsection{Effective Discriminable Depth}
57	\label{sec:atlas:edd}
58	
59	Aggregate accuracy masks the fact that adjacent depths become harder to distinguish as depth increases.
60	We quantify this via the \emph{Effective Discriminable Depth} (EDD).
61	For each adjacent depth pair $(d, d{+}1)$, we train a dedicated binary RF (500 trees, 5-fold stratified CV) and compute $\mathrm{AUC}(d, d{+}1)$---the ROC AUC of this pairwise classifier at separating depth-$d$ from depth-$(d{+}1)$ samples.
62	Given a discrimination threshold $T$, EDD is defined as:
63	\[
64	\mathrm{EDD}_T = \max \bigl\{ d^*{+}1 : \mathrm{AUC}(d^*, d^*{+}1) \geq T \bigr\}
65	\]
66	where $d^* \in \{0, 1, \dots, K{-}1\}$.
67	Intuitively, $\mathrm{EDD}_T$ is the deepest generation depth at which the classifier can still reliably separate it from the preceding depth; beyond this boundary, adjacent depths become statistically indistinguishable.
68	We set $T{=}0.75$ as the default threshold---the midpoint between chance AUC (0.5) and perfect discrimination (1.0)---and report sensitivity at $T \in \{0.65, 0.70, 0.75, 0.80\}$ below.
69	
70	Within the 4-class atlas (d0--d3), 7 of 9 cells achieve $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=3$; the two exceptions are Pythia and OLMo with top-$k$ sampling ($\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=2$), consistent with top-$k$'s signal-attenuating effect noted in \S\ref{sec:atlas:indist}.
71	
72	To probe deeper, we extend the Pythia-1.4B nucleus pipeline to 6 depths (d0--d5) and train a 500-tree binary RF for each adjacent pair (we increase from 200 to 500 trees for binary classifiers to ensure convergence on the smaller per-pair sample sizes).
73	Figure~\ref{fig:auc_decay} shows the pairwise AUC decay curve.
74	The d0\,vs.\,d1 boundary is near-perfect (AUC\,=\,0.998), reflecting the well-known human--machine gap.
75	Subsequent boundaries degrade monotonically: d1\,vs.\,d2 achieves 0.925 [95\% CI: 0.914,\,0.935], d2\,vs.\,d3 reaches 0.823 [0.805,\,0.840], and d3\,vs.\,d4 yields 0.784 [0.765,\,0.802]---with the confidence interval lower bound still exceeding $T{=}0.75$.
76	At d4\,vs.\,d5, AUC drops to 0.683 [0.661,\,0.705], falling below the threshold and fixing $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$.
77	
78	\begin{figure}[t]
79	\centering
80	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_auc_decay.pdf}
81	\caption{Binary pairwise AUC between adjacent depths (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$, d0--d5). Shaded bands: 95\% bootstrap CI. Dashed line: $T{=}0.75$. $\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$: depth pairs up to d3\,vs.\,d4 remain discriminable.}
82	\label{fig:auc_decay}
83	\end{figure}
84	
85	Table~\ref{tab:edd_sensitivity} shows the sensitivity of EDD to the choice of threshold.
86	At $T{=}0.65$, all pairs through d4\,vs.\,d5 pass, giving $\mathrm{EDD}=5$.
87	At $T{=}0.80$, only d1\,vs.\,d2 and d2\,vs.\,d3 pass, yielding $\mathrm{EDD}=3$.
88	The d0\,vs.\,d1 pair passes at every threshold ($\text{AUC}\geq0.99$).
89	
90	\begin{table}[t]
91	\centering
92	\small
93	\caption{EDD sensitivity to threshold $T$ (Pythia-1.4B, nucleus $p{=}0.95$). \checkmark: pairwise AUC $\geq T$; ---: below $T$. d0\,vs.\,d1 always passes (AUC$\geq$0.99) and is omitted.}
94	\label{tab:edd_sensitivity}
95	\begin{tabular}{cccccr}
96	\toprule
97	$T$ & d1/d2 & d2/d3 & d3/d4 & d4/d5 & EDD \\
98	\midrule
99	0.65 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & 5 \\
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016PD8yupw1UXWgR3GXGi44D`</summary>

```text
105	\paragraph{Z-score normalization does not resolve the inversion.}
106	A natural hypothesis is that per-feature z-score normalization might correct this covariate shift \cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting}.
107	For RF, z-normalization leaves accuracy essentially unchanged (59.1\% $\to$ 59.2\%) and does not improve d1v2 AUC ($\Delta < 0.02$), as expected since tree splits depend on feature rank order, not scale.
108	Z-score standardization improves overall logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy from 25\% to 61\%, with d1v2 AUC gains for nucleus and top-$k$ holdouts (0.61--0.89).
109	However, it does not recover the temperature-induced d1v2 inversion---AUC worsens for two of three models (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\to$0.19; Pythia 0.52), confirming that temperature decoding induces a genuine geometry-level shift in feature space rather than a correctable scale difference.
110	
```

```json
  "new_string": "At d4\\,vs.\\,d5, AUC drops to 0.683 [0.661,\\,0.705], falling below the threshold and fixing $\\mathrm{EDD}_{0.75}=4$.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model EDD.}\nWe extend the same d0--d5 pipeline to OLMo-1B and GPT-2~XL (both nucleus $p{=}0.95$).\nThe d3\\,vs.\\,d4 AUC consistently clusters near the 0.75 threshold across all three models: Pythia 0.784 ($p{=}0.001$), OLMo 0.765 ($p{=}0.091$), GPT-2~XL 0.750 ($p{=}0.505$).\nOnly Pythia's d3\\,vs.\\,d4 boundary is statistically significant; OLMo is marginal and GPT-2~XL is not.\nAt d4\\,vs.\\,d5, all three models fall below 0.70 (range 0.681--0.694), uniformly failing the $T{=}0.75$ threshold.\nThis graded pattern---consistent directional decline with model-dependent statistical strength at the boundary---supports EDD as a robust summary metric while highlighting that the exact boundary depth should be interpreted with per-model confidence intervals.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]"
}
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Generalization and Robustness}\n\\label{sec:generalization}\n\nThe atlas (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas}) established in-distribution accuracy of 69.6--85.4\\%.\nWe now test whether these results reflect genuine statistical regularities or artifacts of specific training conditions, evaluating four axes: classifier choice (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:baselines}), cross-model transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossmodel}), cross-strategy transfer (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:crossstrategy}), and end-to-end learned representations (\\S\\ref{sec:gen:dcol}).\n\n\\subsection{Baseline Comparison}\n\\label{sec:gen:baselines}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares classification methods on the Pythia-1.4B nucleus setting.\nThree feature-based methods---Random Forest (RF), CORAL ordinal regression \\cite{coral2020}, and DivEye \\cite{diveye2026}---all operate on the same 19 statistical features and achieve statistically indistinguishable accuracy (78.1--78.2\\%; pairwise bootstrap $p>0.05$ after Bonferroni correction, 95\\% CI within $\\pm$1.1pp).\nThese three significantly outperform CE-MLP ($p<0.001$, $+$3.8pp), which in turn trails logistic regression by $\\sim$0.1pp.\nSingle-feature regression on mean perplexity performs at chance (25.1\\%), confirming that depth estimation requires the multi-dimensional feature representation defined in \\S\\ref{sec:method}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison of classification methods on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF, CORAL, and DivEye use the same 19 statistical features and are statistically indistinguishable (bootstrap $p>0.05$, Bonferroni-corrected). Chance = 25\\%. 5-fold CV std: 0.2--0.9pp across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 \\\\\nCORAL & 78.1 \\\\\nDivEye & 78.2 \\\\\nCE-MLP & 74.9 \\\\\nLR & 75.0 \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot)\\footnotemark & 40.0 \\\\\nppl-regression (single feature) & 25.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\footnotetext{Evaluated on 200 samples (approximately 50 per class) from the Pythia-1.4B nucleus cell only, due to API cost; 95\\% CI: $\\pm$6.8pp. All other methods use the full 20{,}000-sample set.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThat three structurally different classifiers (tree ensemble, ordinal regression, diversity-based) achieve identical performance on the same features confirms that the discriminative signal resides in the 19 statistical features, not the classifier architecture.\nA zero-shot LLM baseline (DeepSeek V3.2) achieves 40.0\\%---above chance but 38 points below feature-based methods.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:confusion_matrix} shows that d0 is near-perfectly identified in both settings; in-distribution errors concentrate at the d2/d3 boundary, while cross-model transfer spreads confusion more broadly across d1--d3.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_confusion_matrix.pdf}\n\\caption{Confusion matrices for the best in-distribution setting (GPT-2 XL, temp $\\tau{=}0.9$, 85.4\\%) and the best cross-model leave-one-out transfer (OLMo holdout, 69.2\\%). d0 recall exceeds 99\\% in both; errors spread across the d1--d3 range under transfer.}\n\\label{fig:confusion_matrix}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossmodel}\n\nTo test whether depth fingerprints generalize across architectures, we perform leave-one-model-family-out evaluation: train on two model families (nucleus sampling only) and test on the held-out third.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model} reports results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model transfer (leave-one-out, nucleus only). Train on 2 model families, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Acc & d0 & d1 & d2 & d3 \\\\\n & (\\%) & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. & Rec. \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia & 62.4 & 100.0 & 49.3 & 26.8 & 73.6 \\\\\nOLMo & \\textbf{69.2} & 99.3 & 75.5 & 43.3 & 58.8 \\\\\nGPT-2 XL & 67.6 & 98.5 & 74.7 & 52.6 & 44.5 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & 66.4 & 99.3 & 66.5 & 40.9 & 59.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean transfer accuracy reaches 66.4\\% (2.7$\\times$ chance).\nThe per-class breakdown reveals that d0 recall ($>$98\\%) inflates headline accuracy; d2 recall drops as low as 26.8\\% (Pythia holdout), making it the most confusable class.\nPythia is the hardest holdout (62.4\\%) and OLMo the easiest (69.2\\%).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Strategy Transfer}\n\\label{sec:gen:crossstrategy}\n\nWe next train on two decoding strategies and test on the held-out third.\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_strategy} reports per-model results.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-strategy transfer (leave-one-out). Train on 2 strategies, test on held-out 3rd. Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:cross_strategy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nHoldout & Pythia & OLMo & GPT-2 XL \\\\\n & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) & Acc (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nnucleus & \\textbf{69.4} & \\textbf{65.4} & \\textbf{59.9} \\\\\ntemp ($\\tau{=}0.9$) & 62.8 & 58.2 & 56.1 \\\\\ntop-$k$ ($k{=}50$) & 51.5 & 54.9 & 53.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMean cross-strategy accuracy (59.1\\%) falls 7 points below cross-model transfer (66.4\\%), indicating that strategy-induced distribution shifts pose a greater generalization challenge than architectural differences.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature-induced boundary inversion.}\nTraining on nucleus + top-$k$ and testing on temperature yields d1v2 AUC of 0.129 for GPT-2~XL and 0.253 for OLMo---both below 0.5, indicating that the d1/d2 decision boundary \\emph{inverts} (Pythia retains a degraded 0.576).\nThis follows from the trajectory analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:atlas}: temperature sampling shifts feature values at depth $d$ toward depth $d{+}1$ under nucleus/top-$k$.\n\n\\paragraph{Z-score normalization does not resolve the inversion.}\nA natural hypothesis is that per-feature z-score normalization might correct this covariate shift \\cite{sugiyama2007_importance_weighting}.\nFor RF, z-normalization leaves accuracy essentially unchanged (59.1\\% $\\to$ 59.2\\%) and does not improve d1v2 AUC ($\\Delta < 0.02$), as expected since tree splits depend on feature rank order, not scale.\nZ-score standardization improves overall logistic regression cross-strategy accuracy from 25\\% to 61\\%, with d1v2 AUC gains for nucleus and top-$k$ holdouts (0.61--0.89).\nHowever, it does not recover the temperature-induced d1v2 inversion---AUC worsens for two of three models (GPT-2~XL 0.50$\\to$0.24, OLMo 0.50$\\to$0.19; Pythia 0.52), confirming that temperature decoding induces a genuine geometry-level shift in feature space rather than a correctable scale difference.\n\n\\subsection{End-to-End Learning}\n\\label{sec:gen:dcol}\n\nTo test whether pretrained language models can learn depth signals directly from raw text, we finetune DeBERTa-v3-base and RoBERTa-base end-to-end (lr$\\,{=}\\,2{\\times}10^{-5}$, 3 epochs, 5-fold CV) on Pythia-1.4B nucleus data.\nTable~\\ref{tab:e2e_baselines} summarizes the comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end baseline comparison on Pythia-1.4B nucleus. RF uses 19 hand-crafted statistical features; PLM baselines are finetuned on raw text. 5-fold CV (std where shown). Chance = 25\\%.}\n\\label{tab:e2e_baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & 4-class Acc (\\%) & $\\Delta$ vs RF \\\\\n\\midrule\nRF (19 features) & 78.2 & --- \\\\\nRoBERTa-base finetune & 59.5 $\\pm$ 3.2 & $-$18.7pp \\\\\nDeBERTa-v3-base finetune & 58.3 $\\pm$ 4.7 & $-$19.9pp \\\\\nDeepSeek V3.2 (zero-shot) & 40.0 & $-$38.2pp \\\\\nChance & 25.0 & $-$53.2pp \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth PLM architectures---masked language modeling (RoBERTa) and disentangled attention (DeBERTa)---converge to ${\\sim}$59\\%, with the 1.2pp inter-backbone gap ruling out architecture selection bias.\nBoth easily separate d0 (F1$>$0.95) but fail at adjacent-depth discrimination (d1/d2 F1$<$0.42), precisely where \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} and \\textsc{var\\_surprisal} excel.\nDCOL \\cite{cloc2025} with RoBERTa-large fared worse (best 64.5\\%, 5/6 runs unstable).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature overlap analysis.}\nProbing reveals a significant negative correlation between DeBERTa attention importance and RF permutation importance (Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.484$, $p{=}0.036$; top-5 Jaccard overlap$\\,{=}\\,0$).\nPLM representations excel at surface lexical features (TTR: $R^2{=}0.85$, repeated bigrams) but fail to capture perplexity distribution statistics---\\textsc{var\\_surprisal} and \\textsc{p90\\_ppl} yield negative $R^2$ under linear probing.\nThis confirms that hand-crafted statistical features capture depth signals that PLMs cannot learn from raw text alone, particularly the distributional tail properties that constitute the dominant depth markers (\\S\\ref{sec:atlas:features}).",
```

1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director instructs the Paper Agent to write, refine, and patch an EMNLP 2026 paper (ARR May cycle, DDL May 25) for project "recursive_gen_depth_est". The paper's core claim is that recursive generation leaves quantifiable statistical fingerprints enabling depth estimation via 19 lightweight features. This conversation window covers Step 5 Iterative Refinement (Reviewer Rounds 2-4 achieving convergence), followed by multiple Patch-mode rounds incorporating reviewer feedback, new experiment data (dual backbone end-to-end baselines, cross-model EDD), and narrative restructuring (baseline statistical significance, feature universality emphasis). Communication with Director is exclusively via `paper_agent_post_to_director`.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - Recursive generation depth estimation: ordinal classification over model→data→model cycles (d0=human, d1-d3=machine)
   - 19 statistical features: 9 perplexity stats + 3 surprisal stats + 4 lexical diversity + 3 repetition
   - EDD (Effective Discriminable Depth): max depth where adjacent binary pairwise AUC ≥ threshold T; EDD₀.₇₅=4
   - EDD formula corrected: `max{d*+1 : AUC(d*, d*+1) ≥ T}` (was `max{d*}`)
   - Cross-model EDD: Pythia d3v4=0.784 (p=0.001, significant), OLMo d3v4=0.765 (p=0.091, marginal), GPT-2 XL d3v4=0.750 (p=0.505, not significant); all d4v5 <0.70
   - Dual backbone end-to-end baseline: DeBERTa-v3-base 58.3±4.7%, RoBERTa-base 59.5±3.2%, 19pp below feature-based 78.2%
   - Feature overlap: DeBERTa attention vs RF permutation importance Spearman ρ=−0.484 (p=0.036), Jaccard=0
   - Bootstrap significance: RF/CORAL/DivEye statistically indistinguishable (p>0.05 Bonferroni), all significantly above CE-MLP (p<0.001, +3.8pp)
   - Temperature-induced d1/d2 AUC inversion: z-score normalization worsens all three models (GPT-2 XL 0.50→0.24, OLMo 0.50→0.19, Pythia 0.63→0.52)
   - Permutation importance validates Gini: p90_ppl top-2 in 9/9 cells, var_surprisal top-2 in 8/9, Gini-permutation ρ=0.94
   - EMNLP page limit: 8 pages main matter; Limitations/references/appendix unlimited
